740
CSE at IITK – Some Recollections from those Early Days
V. Rajaraman (Professor, EE/CSE IITK, 1963-82)
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
The IBM 1620 was the first computer to be installed at IIT/Kanpur. It was a gift from the Kanpur Indo-American Program (KIAP) and arrived in July 1963. The corner room in the Western Labs was earmarked for its installation. When the computer came on a bullock cart and unloaded, we found that the door leading to the computer room was not broad enough to move it into the room. The wall at the far-end was broken and the computer was moved into the room which already had a false floor, false roof, air-conditioning etc. Professors Harry Huskey, Forman Acton, and Irving Rabinowitz had come earlier to help run the computer. The computer was installed by Indian IBM customer engineers who had been trained for this purpose. IBM 1620 was the first computer with a FORTRAN compiler to be installed in India. No other IIT had a computer in 1963.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
I started teaching TA 306, the first course in computer programming in 1964 on the 1620. The computer had 60,000 digits of memory and did decimal arithmetic by looking up an arithmetic table stored in its memory. Then IBM 1620’s informal name was CADET and people joked that it stood for “Can’t Add; Doesn’t Even Try!”  The input used decks of punched cards. The output was also a deck of punched cards that were printed off-line using an accounting machine as a printer. Programming was done using FORGO, a version of FORTRAN II.
The IBM 1620 at IIT/Kanpur. R.N. Basu is mounting a tape. IBM Engineer Hinduja is standing in front of the console.
M.N. Misra is operating the console typewriter. Source: IIT Kanpur 1965 Convocation Publication, IITK and CMU Archives
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
741
Students wrote their program, went to a card punch, punched the program, assembled the cards as a deck and submitted the deck to the operator. The operator collected all the decks from the students, made a batch of programs, and fed it to IBM 1620. The computer ran the programs and the results were punched on cards. Students collected their results and printed them, only to find that the result was an error message saying missing comma in line number 7! After correction, the deck was re-submitted. Hopefully, there were no more errors. Else, the cycle was repeated till the results came through. Finally, if there were no logical errors in the program, the answer was correct. Most students had to go through three rounds of submitting decks, debugging, re-punching, and re-submitting, before they got the correct answer, even for a simple program such as finding the largest of 10 numbers. At the end of the first laboratory class a student came to me and innocently said: Sir, you said our computer is a wonderful machine that will reduce the work I have to do to solve problems. Now I don’t know whether I am working for the computer or the computer is working for me!
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
Working on a deck of computer cards. Prof Irving Rabinowitz is visible on the left, and R.N. Basu, a software engineer with the IITK Computer Centre,
is visible in the middle. Picture: Prof Irving Rabinowitz, from the KIAP photo collection of Prof Gio Wiederhold
By 1965, the 1620 was running 24 hours a day, 7 days a week and was saturated. Waiting time to get results from the computer was becoming too long and IIT/Kanpur started looking for a bigger computer. KIAP did not have a budget allocation to buy a larger computer and we had to find our own resources to make this purchase. Besides this, getting foreign exchange to buy computers was difficult.
Meanwhile, the Tata Institute of Fundamental Research (TIFR) in Bombay was allocated funds by the Government of India to buy a large mainframe computer. IBM was very confident that it would get the order from TIFR and had quoted an IBM 7044. However, TIFR decided to buy a CDC 3600 from Control Data Corporation instead and IBM was left holding an unsold 7044.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
742
When IBM learnt that we were planning to buy a larger computer, it approached us with a proposal to sell the IBM 7044. The 7044 was a mainframe computer with 32 Kilo words (36 bits per word) main memory, 4 tape drives, a fast CPU, and I/O units, but it was already being phased out in the US by IBM and succeeded by the IBM 360 series. Gio Wiederhold, a visiting KIAP Professor, was given the task of negotiating with IBM and getting a unit with the latest upgrades, to ensure that it lasted us for a while.  The normal practice of IBM was to quote the price of imported computers in US Dollars. We told IBM our difficulty in getting foreign exchange. IBM, after consultation with their management, offered to sell the machine for Rs.60 Lakhs after giving a 60% educational discount.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
IIT/Kanpur, however, did not have Rs. 60 Lakhs in its budget which was a huge amount in 1965. After further discussions, IBM agreed to receive Rs. 60 Lakhs in five installments of Rs.12 Lakhs each year. This offer was given in writing by one of the sales managers of IBM India in early 1966. This offer was satisfactory. However, the new computer centre building was not ready and there was no space to put the huge 7044 in the Western Laboratory. IIT/ Kanpur allocated Rs.12 lakhs to be paid in the financial year 1966-67. The 7044 was to be delivered in the later part of 1966.
We then received a shock. The Rupee was devalued on 6 June 1966 from Rs. 4.76 to a dollar to Rs.7.50 to a dollar. IBM India reneged and told us that the deal was off. Prof. Forman Acton of Princeton University, who was a member of the first American team that supervised the installation of IBM 1620, had stayed in contact with IITK. We told him about our problem with IBM. He immediately shot off a letter, in his own inimitable style, to one of the senior Vice-Presidents of IBM in the USA (who incidentally had been Acton’s student at Princeton) that what IBM India was doing was unethical and they could not go back on a written commitment. Acton got a reply that IBM would stand by its obligations. The IBM India sales manager came back and agreed to sell IBM 7044 as per the original agreement.  The 7044 was delivered in mid-July and became operational in September 1966.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
Professor Forman Acton (Princeton University) was part of the original team that installed the IBM-1620 in 1963-64.  He stayed in contact with IITK and returned for another semester-long visit in August 1967. Picture: IITK archives.
743
Use of IBM 7044 as a stand-alone computer was inefficient as Input/Output was too slow compared to its processing speed. An IBM 1401 was required as an Input/Output computer. The 1401 read punched cards input and stored the program/data on a tape. This tape was read by IBM 7044 and the computed result was written on another tape. This was read by the IBM 1401 and printed on a high-speed line printer. The 1401 was made in India by IBM and we bought it the next year. It had 4 Tape drives, 5 Disk drives (2MB/drive), a card reader, and a high-speed line printer. It cost around Rs.35 Lakhs.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
The IBM 1620 was moved from the Western Lab to the New Computer Centre Building after 7044/1401 started working in 1967.  Eventually, it was sold to Steel Authority of India R&D Division, Ranchi around 1970. Dr. S.C. Mehta who was on the faculty at IIT/K joined SAIL R&D and was using it in their research.
The IBM 7044 and IBM 1401 were the main computer systems at IIT/Kanpur from 1966 till the DEC-1090 arrived in 1979. IIT/Kanpur was able to sell these computers by auction, a month before the DEC-1090 arrived, for Rs. 45 Lakhs. How we did it is another story!
The IIT Kanpur Computer Room, c. 1977.  The IBM-7044 is visible in the back, and the IBM-1401 on the right.
The student in the room is Prasanna Mulgaonkar.  Picture: Shirish Joshi (BT, ChE, 1973-78)
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
Originally published in The Spark, Issue 2, December 2021, pg. 40-44, available at:
Issue 2
For additional details on the arrival of the IBM-1620 please refer to The Spark, Issue 1, October 2021,
pg. 4-14, available at: Issue 1
744
Establishment of the CSE Academic Programmes at IIT/Kanpur
V. Rajaraman (Professor EE/CSE, 1963-82)
Prologue
I joined IIT/Kanpur as an Assistant Professor in the Department of EE on March 23, 1963. I was an Assistant Professor of Statistics at the University of Wisconsin, Madison before I came to Kanpur. My Ph.D. was in EE (Adaptive Control Systems). When I joined IITK there was no campus. We were in a wing of HBTI in Agricultural Gardens (called Company Bagh by locals). We officially moved to the Kalyanpur campus at the end of March 1963. The only building which was ready was the workshop. First, the library moved to the workshop. About a month later, the departments moved to the workshop. I remember the May - June heat in the workshop where all faculty of EE sat together in one enclosure. The next buildings to be ready were the Southern and Western Labs. The IBM 1620 moved to the far end of the Western Laboratory in August 1963.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
The (Late) Professor H.K. Kesavan joined as Professor and Head of the EE Department in early 1964. Before joining IITK he was the Head of the EE Department at the University of Waterloo in Canada. It was fortunate that he joined IITK as he was far-sighted and an excellent academic administrator. He was my mentor in my early days at IITK and I learnt a lot from him about academic administration. There was only a BTech programme when Kesavan joined. He was convinced that with a young research-oriented faculty joining the EE department it was necessary to start a post-graduate programme as soon as possible.
Prof. HK Kesavan giving Mrs. Indira Gandhi a tour of the IIT Kanpur Television Centre, 1966.
Source: EC Subbarao, “An Eye for Excellence: First Fifty Innovative years of IIT Kanpur
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
745
IITK was a new institute and it was necessary to publicise its special facilities and the young talented faculty.  On Prof. Kesavan’s suggestion, I went to IIT/Madras and IISc, Bangalore in March 1964 to talk to graduating students in EE and tried to convince them to join the MTech programme being started in IITK in August 1964. The first batch of ten students joined in August 1964. Among them was H.V. Sahasrabuddhe who had earlier joined IITK as a research assistant after graduating from IIT/Bombay, S.C. Seth, C.V.S. Rao, and R.K. Ragade from IISc, Bangalore. (All three went on to get Phds.)
In early 1965 H.N. Mahabala joined the EE Department from the University of Waterloo. He had worked with a Bendix computer at the University Of Saskatchewan in Canada where he obtained a Ph.D. In August 1964, Professor Kesavan was made the Head of the Computer Centre in addition to his responsibilities as Head of EE. He requested me to join him in the Computer Centre as his deputy and I gladly accepted. I had taken courses in digital computation and programmed the IBM 704 in the USA during my student days and had extensively worked with analog computers during my doctoral research. In 1965 Mahabala and I started teaching some courses in computers and requested Kesavan to allow us to introduce an option in MTech EE with specialisation in Computer Science. He was far-sighted and saw the future growth of computing and the unique position of IITK that had a computer. It was an informal arrangement as the MTech degree was awarded in EE.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
Starting an M.Tech. (Computer Science) Option in the EE Department
The Computer Science option in EE became very popular and a majority of students admitted to MTech EE opted to take courses in Computer Science and do a project in that area creating some friction among the EE Department faculty. The courses in Computer Science were limited. They were on Digital Logic Design, Software Engineering, Programming Languages, Artificial Intelligence, and Design of Information Systems. Approximately a dozen students were graduating with MTech with the Computer Science option in EE.  Ph.D. students also joined EE in 1965/66. If I remember right H.V. Sahasrabuddhe started working with Professor H.K. Kesavan as a Ph.D. student in 1966 in the area of power systems that involved considerable programming using the 1620.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
In 1966, P.C.P. Bhatt who was a lecturer and C.R. Muthukrishnan started working on their Ph.D. thesis with my guidance. Muthukrishnan submitted his thesis in 1969 working in the area of Decision Tables and joined the faculty of EE along with Sahasrabuddhe. Another Ph.D. student was (Late) T. Radhakrishnan who worked with me in the area of parallel information retrieval. He also joined the EE faculty. P.C.P. Bhatt also obtained a Ph.D. degree in 1969. We now had six faculty members in the Computer Science area. In 1968, Professor Kesavan left IITK and returned to the University of Waterloo where he started a new Systems Engineering Department. I took over as the head of the Computer Centre.
Starting an Interdisciplinary Post-Graduate Programme in Computer Science
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
In 1971 the Senate of IITK approved starting separate MTech programmes in Computer Science, Materials Science, and Nuclear Engineering. The first admissions to a new MTech Computer Science Programme were started with an admission of around a dozen students. As an interdisciplinary programme the minimum qualification for admission was BTech in any discipline or an MSc degree in Mathematics or Physics.
746
The parting of ways from the EE Department was cordial. The EE faculty was happy that Computer Science got separated as the MTech students in EE, after the split, were taking EE courses and working on their MTech and Ph.D. theses in the traditional EE subjects such as Power Engineering, Electronics, Control Systems, Electromagnetics, and Circuit Theory.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
I continued to be a faculty in EE in addition to being the Convenor of the Computer Science Programme.  The first MTech (Computer Science) batch graduated in 1973 and most of the graduating students were employed by TCS. The course was in great demand and we continued to get good students. Admission was by a written test and an interview. GATE had not started.
The Computer Science faculty size increased slowly and steadily with Shyam Kumar, S.V. Rangaswami, S.K. Basu, Narsingh Deo, R.M.K. Sinha, V.K. Vaishnavi, S.N. Maheshwari, Arvind (Visiting), S.C. Seth (Visiting), R. Kodandapani, K.V. Nori, and R. Sankar joining over the years.  IIT/Madras installed an IBM 370/155 system in 1973 and did not have any experienced faculty to manage the computer centre. Mahabala accepted an invitation from IIT/Madras to join its faculty, manage the Computer Centre, and also start an academic programme in Computer Science. He persuaded Muthukrishnan and Radhakrishnan to join him. P.C.P. Bhatt was invited by IIT/Delhi to start its Computer Science programme.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
An early picture of the CSE Programme Faculty, in the early 1970s, received from Prof M.S. Krishnamoorthy.
This photo was taken after a Quality Improvement Program Course - an intensive course aimed at improving the quality of faculty members
in other Engineering Colleges. Seated (L to R) are a Program Attendee (QIPA), M.S. Krishnamoorthy, QIPA, V. Rajaraman, R.M.K. Sinha,
QIPA, and H.V. Sahasrabuddhe.
Standing in the back are additional QIP attendees along with V. M. Malhotra and V. K. Kaithal who were the Teaching Assistants for that course
747
The MTech and Ph.D. programmes thrived. IITK was graduating around a dozen MTechs every year. The Ph.D. programme was also thriving. In my memory several students including A.S. Sethi, V.K. Vaishnavi, M.S. Krishnamoorthy, R.M.K. Sinha, V.Gupta, M. Ibramsha, Om Vikas, B.K. Gairola, Asha Goel, V.M. Malhotra, R. Govindarajulu, S. Krishna, and S. Biswas obtained Ph.D. during the period 1970 – 1982. (This list is not exhaustive).
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
The IITK CSE Faculty with the MTech graduating class of 1978.
Seen here (L to R) are Row 1: Professors A.S. Sethi, Arvind (visiting from MIT),
V. Rajaraman, R. Sankar, H.V. Sahasrabuddhe, M.S. Krishnamoorthy; Row 2: T. Sanyal, Agarwal, Murthy,
Lt. Gyan Prakash, Ashok Modi, Flt. Lt. Routela, Gorthi, Madhav Prabhu; Row 3:  Alok Khare, Satish Goyal, Satpal,
Laxman Badiga, Mukkamala Ravi, M. Pramod Kumar Rao, R. R. Gargeya, Ambrish Mathur, Bhasker
Picture shared by Professors Hari Sahasrabuddhe and M S Krishnamoorthy
Introducing the B.Tech. Programme in Computer Science
By the mid-1970s, the faculty of the Computer Science programme felt that CS was maturing as a discipline, and the time was ripe to start an undergraduate programme. As the convenor of the CS programme, I prepared a proposal, after consulting the faculty, to start a BTech programme in Computer Science. I discussed this proposal with the Acting Director Dr. Jagdish Lal in 1975. He was convinced and as Chairman of the IITK senate introduced the proposal in a senate meeting. There was extensive discussion in the senate with many members opposing the resolution.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
748
The arguments against its introduction included thoughts that: it was not a major discipline; had little content besides programming; was already being offered as programming diplomas by private companies; would not offer employment for BTech graduates; and was not an Engineering course.  Finally, other departments would have had to give up some seats as the total BTech intake was fixed by the Government of India.
I and my Computer Science colleagues in the Senate pointed out that it was indeed a mature discipline; it was not just programming; there were professional societies on computers in India, the UK, the USA, Japan, and several countries in Europe; there was an International Federation of Information Processing Societies (IFIPS); it was growing rapidly, and would become essential knowledge for all engineers.   Many countries in the world including the UK, the USA, and several countries in Europe already had Computer Science departments in their universities.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
Despite our attempts to convince the senate members, no decision was taken. Dr. Jagdish Lal left IITK in 1976 and returned as the Principal of Motilal Nehru Regional Engineering College (MNREC), Allahabad, from where he had come to IITK.  While the IITK senate was continuing the debate on the BTech programme in Computer Science, Dr. Lal convinced the Board of Governors of MNREC and started a BTech programme in Computer Science in 1976 two years before IITK did, thus becoming the first institute in the country to offer this.
In 1976, Dr. A. Bhattacharya took over as the new Director of IITK. I had a long discussion with him and he was convinced of the importance of starting a BTech programme in CSE. He re-introduced the proposal in the senate. The arguments for and against were repeated. Finally, Dr. Bhattacharya steered the discussion successfully and the senate approved a BTech programme in Computer Science with a student strength of 20 in 1977. He also steered the proposal through the Board of Governors. The programme was slated to begin in 1978. A few seats were taken from other departments and allocated to the BTech programme in Computer Science.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
BTech Programme Begins
BTech in Computer Science was announced as a discipline in the Joint Entrance Examination (JEE) of 1978. Many parents came to me to discuss Computer Science and its prospects before choosing the department for their children. I distinctly remember Rajeev Motwani’s father and Jaideep Srivastava’s father who were anxious about the future of Computer Science and had long discussions with me. Both Rajeev and Jaideep joined the first batch of the BTech Computer Science programme.
To the surprise of the entire faculty of IITK and the other IITs, BTech Computer Science became the first choice of a large number of students who qualified in the JEE. By 1980, the last student admitted to the program had an All-India Rank of 40! The students from the first (1978) batch were uniformly good and many of them distinguished themselves as world leaders in Computer Science.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
749
The IITK CSE Department circa 1982, with many of the faculty and members of the first B. Tech. batch present.
Seated on chairs in the second row are Prof. Chandrasekhar, Prof. RMK Sinha, Prof. H.V. Sahasrabuddhe,
Mrs. Veena Sahasrabuddhe, Mrs. Dharma Rajaraman, Prof. V. Rajaraman, Prof. R. Sankar, Mrs. Padmavathy Sankar,
Prof. Somenath Biswas, and Mrs. Kiran Biswas.
Picture shared by CK Mohan (seen in the middle of the front row), and Prof. V Rajaraman
Epilogue
A professor of IIT/Kharagpur collared me after a meeting in Delhi and asked how IITK could give a BTech degree in Computer Science when there was no department. I retorted that IITK was a pioneer that broke many existing norms. IITK was the first IIT to have a core curriculum, letter grades, grading “on a curve”, a semester system, sizeable humanities courses in the BTech, etc.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
When the other IITs saw the success of IITK’s BTech programme in Computer Science, they unitedly requested
the Ministry of Human Resources Development to allow them to start a Department of Computer Science and Engineering.
The ministry agreed in 1983 and all IITs started a CSE Department. The Computer Science Programme at IITK became the CSE Department in 1984.
I was not at IITK when the new department started as I had left IITK to join IISc in 1982.
Originally published in The Spark, Issue 3, April 2022, pg. 79-83, available at:

With their inputs, IITK defined the Undergraduate CSE curriculum, with the first batch joining in 1978, and graduating in 1983.  Here are some images clicked in Bengaluru, August 2019.

751
CSE at IITK: The Arrival of the DEC-1090
V. Rajaraman (Professor EE/CSE, 1963-82)
Earlier, I had written the story of the purchase of IBM 7044/1401. These systems became fully operational in 1966 in the new Computer Centre building. From day one they were used 24 hours a day, 7 days a week. Besides this system, there was an IBM 1620 that continued to be used for undergraduate teaching and laboratories until it was sold to SAIL R&D. We also bought an IBM 1800, a small process control computer, for experiments in real-time control.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
The (Late) Professor John McCarthy, head of the Artificial Intelligence Laboratory at Stanford University visited IITK in 1971. He saw that students were using a computer that had a batch processing operating system (OS) which did not allow interactive computing. He was a great believer in interactive time-sharing systems.  He had written a famous memo to the Director of the MIT computer centre in 1959 pointing out the advantages of interactive time-shared use of computers for better human-computer symbiosis that would improve the productivity of computer users. That memo led to the development of the first time-sharing operating system in the world, called Compatible Time-Sharing Operating System (CTSS) in 1961, for the IBM 709 at MIT.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
Professor John McCarthy, Turing Laureate, 1971. Picture: Stanford University archives
Professor McCarthy told me that his laboratory at Stanford had a DEC PDP 1 computer with a time-sharing OS which was in good working condition but was being replaced with a larger computer. He wanted to donate the PDP 1 to IITK. I was at first reluctant as it is normally difficult to maintain a used computer.  On second thought, after consulting my colleagues in the computer centre, I decided that such a machine in the laboratory would allow students to have hands-on experience with both hardware and software of a computer that was not possible with IBM machines used by all departments and maintained by IBM engineers.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
The PDP 1 computer was shipped courtesy of KIAP and arrived at Kanpur in 1972. It was installed in the Computer Centre by our students who were assisted by Professor H.N. Mahabala and it started working. The computer came with only two terminals. One was a graphics terminal and the other a teletype. Several users could not use it simultaneously. Its greatest attraction for students was a large display terminal that came with it. It was used to play one of the early computer games called Star Wars. I gave both hardware and software projects on PDP 1 to many undergraduate students of the EE department. I remember R.S. Nikhil and Bhasker, among others, who worked with it.
752
The PDP 1 at IIT Kanpur. Visible in the picture is MTech student, MV Rao. Picture: Amrit Yegna Narayan
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
A 20 MB disk (one of the largest IBM disks in the 1960s) was added to IBM 7044/1401, but by 1976, it was saturated and IITK wanted to replace it. However, during the 1970s there was a serious balance of payments deficit in India, and the import of computers was highly restricted. IITK’s budget also was not adequate to buy a new machine that would have cost around Rs. 1 Crore.
I was a member of the Electronics Commission of the Government of India, chaired by the Secretary of the Department of Electronics (DoE), now called the Ministry of Electronics and Information Technology (MEITY), from 1978 to 1982. I was also a member of several committees of the DoE that approved buying computers for the Regional Computer Centres being set up by the DoE and computers requested by other organizations during the late 1970s. I was able to convince the Secretary of the DoE of the need to replace the aging IBM 7044/1401 system at IITK. The Secretary agreed to fund IITK up to Rs.50 Lakhs in 1978 provided IITK was able to contribute matching funds from its budget.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
IITK was short of funds, and it was difficult to allocate Rs. 50 Lakhs from its budget for a new computer. I felt that we could raise some money by selling the IBM 7044/1401 computers. As I pointed out earlier, there was a foreign exchange crunch during the latter part of the 1970s. When the Janata Government was in power after the defeat of the Congress party in 1977, George Fernandes was the Industries minister. A new government policy required foreign companies to dilute equity and take an Indian partner. IBM and Coca-Cola refused to comply and were asked to leave India in 1977.
The IBM 1401 was a highly popular computer in India with over 100 companies having 1401 installations. Many of these companies had saturated their 1401s and wanted to expand by renting/buying additional 1401s for their software packages. Their investment in 1401 software was enormous. The IBM plant in India was the only one in the world refurbishing used 1401s and renting/selling them for reuse. IBM had discontinued the sale of 1401 for the rest of the world in 1971. Many Indian companies were thus in a bind with IBM’s exit and I knew that there would be a market for IITK’s 1401. Besides this, IITK had a stockpile of critical spare parts for both 1401 and 7044.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
I told the then Director of IITK, Dr. A. Bhattacharya, that we could raise some funds by selling the 1401 and 7044. He felt that it might be pittance but agreed. Consequently, a committee was appointed that included Srivastava, the then chief accounts officer, and some other professors besides myself.  The committee discussed the procedure to conduct the sale. It was decided to advertise it in the papers and separate tenders were invited for the two computers with an earnest money deposit of Rs. 5 Lakhs each. Many prospective buyers visited IITK to inspect the machines to see whether they were in working condition and also to assess the availability of spare parts. On the day assigned to open the tenders, all the tenderers were present, and the committee met them and explained that their tenders were final and that there would be no negotiation. It was also pointed out that in case a tenderer reneged, the earnest money they had deposited would be forfeited.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
The tenders were opened one by one. There were around 10 bids for 1401 and only one for 7044. The committee was flabbergasted when one of the bids was Rs.30 Lakhs for 1401! The next highest bid was 15 Lakhs. There was only one bid of Rs. 15 Lakhs for 7044 – a much more powerful computer. The highest bid for 1401 and the lone bid for 7044 were from the same company.  This company was owned by two entrepreneurs, Sadasivan and Raghunathan, and was based in Bangalore. Sadasivan was an ex-IBM customer service engineer with many years of experience in maintaining 1401s and Raghunathan was a financier. Sadasivan was confident of maintaining the computers and had assessed that the spare parts IITK was offering were adequate. I learnt that they had already lined up a customer who had contracted to buy two shifts of IBM 1401 time to run their existing programs. They had second thoughts on 7044 and withdrew their offer. The committee explained that they would forfeit Rs. 5 Lakhs, the earnest money deposit.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
753
After the formal tendering process was over, I invited Sadasivan and Raghunathan for a discussion. I asked them why they were withdrawing their bid for the 7044 which was at least 10 times faster than the 1401. Their trepidation was a lack of customers as most companies in India were not familiar with the 7044 and all their software was for the 1401. I then offered to depute programmers from IITK to write software for the 7044 to enable 1401 programs to be run on it much faster. I also assured them that if the software did not work, they could return the 7044. They agreed to take the 7044 and paid Rs. 15 Lakhs. The software was developed in Fortran by our programmers, Nirmal Roberts and K.S. Singh with the guidance of Professor H.V. Sahasrabuddhe. It was good software and ran 1401 programs faster in 7044 than in 1401.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
Dr. Bhattacharya was pleasantly surprised to learn of the sale of the computers for Rs. 45 Lakhs after they had been used for 13 years. We had bought the two machines for around Rs. 95 Lakhs in 1966. The circumstances during 1978-79 were fortuitous for the sale of used mainframe computers in India. Later mainframe computers were sold as junk.
With the time-sharing operating system, the terminals of the DEC-1090 (right) replaced the punch card machines of the IBM-7044 (left).
Visible on the right, in front of the camera, is Rajeev Motwani of the first Undergraduate CSE batch.  Pictures: (L) Echoes In Memory,
IITK photo yearbook, 1982; (R) Growing Up, photo yearbook, 1983
754
The DEC1090 at IITK c. 1979-80. Picture: Clicked by the Late Mahadevan Ramesh (MSc Physics 1972-77),
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]
shared by Murali Monagur.
With Rs. 45 Lakhs from the sale, IITK allocated Rs. 5 Lakhs from its own budget and we were able to match the grant of Rs. 50 Lakhs by the Department of Electronics (DoE). With Rs. 100 Lakhs in the kitty we started exploring the market for a new machine. An evaluation committee was appointed by DoE that invited quotations for a high-end time-sharing computer. Digital Equipment Corporation won the tender. DoE negotiated for two DEC 1090 computers, one for IITK and the other for IISc, Bangalore. IITK had a larger budget and got a bigger DEC 1090 computer. The DEC 1090 was installed in 1979 at the location vacated by IBM 7044/1401.
Originally published in The Spark, Issue 3, April 2022, pg. 89-91, available at:
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 15]

Back – Prof and Mrs Rajaraman at their home at IITK, with a thali of Laddoos to feed the hungry hordes.  Clicked at Holi, 1978.

=== spark issue 17 ===
Source: https://www.iitk.ac.in/dora/spark/issue-17/
Source: Carnegie Mellon University Archives, Pittsburgh, PA822
The tired old anvil on which so many ideas have been hammered into shape,
once more throws off sparks as a new literary idea is forged into form today.
While on the one hand, The Spark is the culmination of intense
thought and action, on the other, it is the beginning of an arduous but
happy journey.
History, it is said, abhors vacuums, and the students of IIT/K abhor it
no less. In a rapidly expanding campus like ours, the gap has been felt
for some time now. To bridge the gap between the various limbs of the
community is the prime objective of The Spark.
Although conceived and sponsored by the students at present, there is no
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
reason why it cannot soon grow and enlarge into a full-fledged community
effort.
Today we give you The Spark, and it is your newsmagazine.
From you must come the support and encouragement that will
nourish and sustain it. We hope that the elder members of the
community will give us their guiding hand and also the patience
and the understanding to face the future cheerfully.
Editorial, The Spark, Vol. 1, No. 1, Feb 15, 1965
Images shared by Dhani Sahai Mathur (BT, ChE, 1961-66)
Digitally restored by Ishan Singh (BT, ChE, 2020-24)

824
Editorial
Five years is just a blip in the scheme of things, but for The Spark to stay relevant for five years in our in-the-moment world says a lot about the love for IITK that resides in the hearts of its alumni. A fun effort started by a couple of bakait alums on social media blossomed into perhaps the biggest repository of memorabilia about IITK, with over six thousand actively participating contributors: a list including current and past students, faculty, technicians, employees, and so many others whose lives have touched IITK.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
The idea of reviving The Spark was born during the COVID years, when participation from a vacated campus was at a standstill. We reached out to the Director (then Prof. Abhay Karandikar) and DORA (then Prof. Jayant K Singh) with the thought of using their help to connect with the alumni diaspora. Per our original communications, we thought that we might be able to publish 8-10 issues of 28-32 pages each. Perhaps, enough for two-three years' worth. Then, we would figure out what to do next.
What followed has exceeded all our expectations, connecting students, alumni, current and former faculty, KIAP families, the campus community, and so many others. As the issues got bigger, we returned to The Spark's original practice of dividing them into sections: campus updates and student contributions, tributes to former faculty, our featured stories, alumni memories, and photo features.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
None of this could have been possible without your contributions. Thank you, dear friends and well-wishers. You are the most important part of this effort. It is you, your interest, contributions and participation that make The Spark what it is. Your letters have shown us that alumni and current IITians across the board appreciate the mix of news, nostalgia and photos in these 21st century issues.
To commemorate this occasion, we reproduce the very first editorial from the very first issue, which appeared in Feb 1965, with the letter announcing the inception of The Spark as 'a new venture of the student group'. So much of that first editorial remains relevant today.
The collage on our front cover is an assembly of the modern Spark, while the back cover shows a set of issues from 1965 to 1969.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
We have changed, clearly, becoming fatter and more colorful. But a passionate commitment to IITK and its community is still our driving force.
Yoga on the Panki Canal. Featuring Ekbal Gajadhar (1973-78) on an inflatable mattress. Amazing balancing skills! Picture: Shirish Joshi.More details on Page 915.

From a Former Editor
Thanks for sending the Spark issue. I immediately went to Chutki Roy's piece, and it brought back some forgotten memories. A few weeks ago, I went through several boxes of stuff we moved from our old house. While organizing a shelf in my garage I found copies of The Spark. Here are the scanned versions. There are eleven files. Only three of them are from my time (1975-80) at IIT. The earliest one is from 1970. Chutki Roy's name appears as an editor in February 1973, though his surname is misspelled in this issue.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
At one time I had the masthead and typewriter that went with the Spark, but they were left behind on campus all those years ago. I skimmed through some of these issues and noticed Dr AP Shukla's name associated with a strike, though it was before my time. I remember his name cropping up at times and a brief internet search showed he became a labor activist, writer, and founder of "Jan Vigyan Samiti" and a leading figure in India's People's Science Movement. Then there was a more recent issue with the candidates for election. I think Anant Menon wrote most of that. Anyway, it featured the late Pravir Dutt.
Manoj Shankar
BT, ChE, 1975-80; Editor, Spark, 1978-79

Remembering Professor PRK Rao
Thank you for the tribute to Professor PRK Rao. The IITK alumni in Hyderabad remember him fondly and we had stayed in touch with him. I share a picture from my visit to Dr. PRK Rao's home with my wife in Oct 2020 on learning that he was experiencing some health issues.
He was delighted to meet us, and we spent about three hours together discussing various technological trends. He was curious to know more about AI / Chatgpt related topics. He showed me many research papers on AI related topics, and we stayed in discussion on AI papers through emails for the next year. It was a surprise to learn that he had quit smoking completely!
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Meeting him was always a blessing.

831
The Inter IIT Cultural Meet 8.0
The Media and Cultural Council
At the heart of IIT Kanpur's creative and artistic ecosystem today is the Media and Cultural (MnC) Council – a student-led body dedicated to fostering creativity, expression, and cultural excellence on campus. Comprising of 14 clubs and 3 societies, the council provides students with platforms to showcase talents across a diverse range of disciplines: music, dance, dramatics, fine arts, photography, filmmaking, literary arts, animation, design, quizzing, fashion, culinary arts, debating, comedy, and more.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
The Historical Legacy
Student cultural activities at IIT Kanpur date back to the earliest days of the institute, predating the establishment of the campus in Kalyanpur. Organized extracurricular activity was governed by the Students' Gymkhana, which came into existence in 1962 and began with a council of student secretaries and a faculty president. A new Gymkhana constitution adopted in 1970 led to the creation of a Student President, the Student Senate, and the establishment of the Cultural and Games Councils and the Students' Films Society, each headed by an elected General Secretary. Through the 1970s and 1980s, the Cultural Council expanded rapidly, encompassing Music, Drama, Literary Arts, Fine Arts, and clubs ranging from Astronomy, HAM Radio, and Auto, to Roller Skating.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Subsequently, a Films and Media Council was added to the Gymkhana which focused on media and content creation. This Council later merged with the Cultural Council to create the Media and Cultural Council that we have today.
The Inter IIT Cultural Meet
The Inter IIT Cultural Meet is an annual celebration of art, creativity, and culture that brings together students from all the IIT campuses. Since its inception, the Cultural Meet has been more than just a competition, it is a festival of ideas, collaboration, and artistic excellence that allows IITians from across the country to connect, perform, and share their diverse traditions and creative expressions on one grand stage.
832
What began as a small initiative for cultural exchange has grown into one of India's most anticipated intercollegiate events. More than a competition, the Meet is a shared journey of learning and expression, from soulful music and powerful theatre to classical performances and dynamic street art, every act tells a story and adds a new shade to this cultural canvas. It is a true celebration of diversity, unity, and the creative spirit that defines every IIT.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
While IITK had podium finishes at the early meets, in recent years its performance had been mixed, finishing 4th or 5th among the 23 teams.
Inter IIT Cultural Meet 8.0
The eighth edition of the Inter IIT Cultural Meet was hosted by IIT Kanpur from December 23–27, 2025. The competition was structured around a General Championship comprising thirteen cups, with each cup containing multiple individual and group events contested over the course of the meet.

Team IITK Motorsports
DESIGN | BUILD | RACE
IITK MOTORSPORTS IS THE AUTOMOTIVE CHAPTER OF INDIAN INSTITUTE OF TECHNOLOGY KANPUR
At IITK Motorsports, we transform classroom concepts into engineering marvels, designing and building high-performance race cars that showcase innovation and technical excellence. Our diverse team brings together students from various engineering disciplines, united by a shared passion and a common goal.
We proudly represent IIT Kanpur at prestigious competitions such as Formula Bharat, Formula Student Italy, SUPRA SAE India, and BAJA SAE India, where we put our skills to the test in environments that mirror industry standards. These events push us to excel, foster teamwork, and drive innovation, qualities that make IITK Motorsports a leader in student engineering.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Composition
The team comprises Junior Technical Members from the second year, Senior Technical Members from the third year, and Team Principals from the fourth year, fostering a strong culture of continuity, collaboration, and knowledge transfer. We are deeply grateful to our Faculty Advisors, Dr. Amarendra Edpuganti and Dr. Twinkle Tripathy, whose constant encouragement, mentorship, and belief in the team have been instrumental in every step of our journey.
Our alumni have played a defining role in shaping Team IITK Motorsports into what it is today. Through their mentorship, guidance, and continued support, they have laid a strong foundation that inspires each new generation to push the boundaries of engineering and innovation. The culture of teamwork, perseverance, and problem-solving fostered within the team has also empowered many of our alumni to go on to build successful ventures across diverse fields, reflecting the lasting impact of their journey with the team.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
839
Organization
Our team consists of six technical subsystems and two non-technical subsystems:
Chassis and Aerodynamics
Designs and manufactures the vehicle structure and aerodynamic package. Focuses on achieving high stiffness, low weight, driver safety, and aerodynamic efficiency.
High Voltage
Responsible for the design, integration, and safety of the high-voltage system. Manages battery packs, power distribution, and compliance with safety regulations.
Brakes and Wheel Assembly
Designs the braking system and wheel-end components, including hubs and uprights. Ensures effective stopping performance, durability, and reliable load transfer to the ground.
Suspension and Steering
Develops systems that control vehicle handling, stability, and feedback. Optimizes contact, suspension kinematics, and steering response for maximum cornering performance.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Powertrain
Develops the system that delivers power to the wheels. Designs and optimizes the drivetrain for maximum performance, efficiency, reliability, vehicle drivability, and power delivery under dynamic racing conditions.
Electronics and Data Acquisition
Designs and integrates the vehicle's electrical architecture, sensors, control systems, and telemetry. Enables reliable vehicle operation while collecting and analyzing data to support testing, validation, and performance optimization.
Finance and Marketing
Manages team finances, sponsorships, and brand outreach. Ensures financial sustainability while building strong industry partnerships and promoting the team's achievements.
Creatives & Web Development
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Manages the team's visual identity and digital presence by designing content for merchandise, jerseys, posters, and social media, while developing and maintaining the team's website and online platforms.
840
Team History
2025 marked 15 years of learning, building, and racing for our team.
Our team was officially founded in 2010 by Pratik Parekh, Kewal Dharamshi, Abhinav Prateek, Rohit A., and Anmol Pandey (left to right in the photograph). By 2013, we were ready with our first Formula Student vehicle, F13, to compete at Formula Student Italy, where we ranked 9th in Cost Presentations and 13th in Business Presentations.
[Left] Founders of the Team, and [Right] Our First Formula Student Chassis
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Alongside Formula Student, we also ventured into All-Terrain Vehicles (ATVs). Our first ATV, B15 brought us the Best Rookie Team Award at BAJA SAEINDIA 2015.
[Left] F13, our first Formula Student Vehicle, and [Right] B15, our first All-terrain vehicle
In 2016, we competed at both SUPRA SAE INDIA with F16 which received the Lightest Vehicle Award, and BAJA SAEINDIA with B16 which earned us the Best Tech-ready team award.
We participated in Formula Bharat 2018 with F18 and secured Overall 14th in the CV category while B19 finished Overall 13th with a remarkable 4-hour Endurance run at BAJA SAEINDIA 2019.
841
[Left] F16, and [Right] B16
[Left] F18, and [Right] B19
Following the COVID-19 pandemic, we transitioned from combustion to Formula Student Electric Vehicles, leveraging years of experience to embrace the next generation of engineering challenges.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
We were the Overall Winners at the Formula Bharat 2023 (Class I). Our first Formula Student Electric Vehicle (FSEV), Vidyut, finished 14th overall at Formula Bharat 2024. Later that year, it emerged as the Overall Champion at Formula Imperial 2024, alongside six additional awards.
[Left] Vidyut(E37) at FB24, and [Right] Cervo(E13) at FB25
842
In 2025, our 2nd-gen FSEV, Cervo, was ranked overall 9th at Formula Bharat 2025 as E13 and Overall 5th EV at SUPRA SAEINDIA 2025 as EV12, with a Gold in the Cost & Manufacturing Event and a Bronze in the Business Event. We also secured the First place in the Rulebook Quiz of Formula Bharat 2026.
[Left] Our Team at Formula Imperial 2024, and [Right] SUPRA SAEINDIA 2025
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Today, we are manufacturing our next-generation Formula Student Electric Vehicle, continuing our pursuit of engineering excellence and innovation with every iteration.
Current Projects
With every new vehicle, we challenge ourselves to push the boundaries of design, engineering, and innovation. We are currently manufacturing the team's third Formula Student Electric Vehicle, incorporating several technical advancements over its predecessors:
Dual-Motor Torque Vectoring: Real-time torque distribution via a custom ECU and planetary gearbox
In-House Aerodynamic Development: Designed and manufactured wings for optimized downforce
Decoupled Suspension: Independent control of ride and roll dynamics for superior handling
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Driverless Vehicle R&D: Driverless groundwork laid through data acquisition and control architecture
Advanced Thermal Management: Integrated cooling system for enhanced accumulator efficiency
In-house Battery Management System: Custom BMS design for flexibility in PCB layout and packaging
Glimpses from our ongoing work

845
Professor M.M. Oberai (1932-2022)
Professor Madan Mohan Oberai was born on September 10, 1932, in Rawalpindi, British India (now in Pakistan). Post partition, he grew up in Delhi where he completed his schooling and then his undergraduate studies from Delhi University.
Fluent in French, he obtained a scholarship to go to France for his graduate studies where he completed his PhD in Fluid Mechanics from the University of Paris in 1963. Subsequently, he accepted an offer from the University of California, Los Angeles (UCLA), where he was an Assistant Professor in Mechanical Engineering from 1963-66.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
At UCLA, a mutual friend introduced him to Rachel Shimkin, a student studying European History and Philosophy and working at the computer centre in the School of Engineering. Madan Mohan and Rachel were married in Los Angeles in 1965. When IIT Kanpur and the Kanpur Indo-American Program (KIAP) beckoned, they moved to India, and Professor Oberai joined the Faculty of Mechanical Engineering in June 1966. Rachel joined the IITK Computer Centre in an operational and administrative capacity. Her most significant contributions included bringing the IBM SHARE software tapes to IITK through KIAP and the US Embassy.
At IITK, Professor Oberai was much admired by the student community and was deeply involved in student and administrative life. He served as Dean of Student Affairs; he was the first Dean of the newly created office of Academic Affairs (1978-80) and was head of the Department of Mechanical Engineering (1987-89). He would regularly take students on industrial tours over the winter breaks and would be present at student celebrations and festivals.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Rachel moved back to the US in 1979 but Prof. Oberai, and their son Amit would stay on at the IITK Campus -- till Amit completed his schooling and then moved to the US for higher studies in 1987. Professor Oberai retired from IITK in September 1992. He initially settled in Delhi near his brother and extended family, before moving to Bhowali, a town next to Nainital, in July 2019. He passed away on February 23, 2022, from complications related to COVID-19, at the age of 89. IITK students remember him as a mentor who genuinely cared about their welfare.
Professors MM Oberai, MA Pai and KR Sarma at IITK, late 1970s. Picture: Shirish Joshi (1973-78)

847
Remembering Professor M. M. Oberai
My first real encounter with Dr. MM Oberai was during my third year (1980-81), when he taught our Fluid Mechanics course. Not a subject which I found endearing, and so avoided working on it, barely scraping through with a C.
But Dr. Oberai always made an impression. Forever smiling, a twinkle of mischief in his eyes, with his spectacles perched on his head, and the classic pipe hanging from his mouth. That's how I remember 'Mamu', our affectionate nickname for him. Dr. Oberai was always smartly dressed and carried himself with a bright and congenial demeanour, but was strict in his actions. He could be the most friendly, but also a bit stern.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
We came across his personal side during our Industrial Tour in December 1981. He accompanied us throughout the tour, on the train, in the buses, along with his young son. He was genial, but managed to somehow keep a whole crowd of young men from being too adventurous. After our tour wound through the TELCOs and Kirloskars in Pune, we landed in Mumbai (Bombay at that time). The day we were getting ready to take the train back to Kanpur, Dr. Oberai announced that he would be taking off now, and that his proteges were old enough to manage the train journey back without an escort, with a warning, in jest, that we better behave ourselves. Five of us (young aspiring Mechanical Engineers) timidly asked if we too could forgo the journey back to Kanpur and take a detour – we were not specific about our plans – and kept them vague – meeting friends etc.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Goa beaches, December 1981. Members of the ME batch of 1978-83 with Prof. Oberai. (L to R): Sudhanshu Goel, Sunil Singh, Manoj Srivastava, Prof. MM Oberai, Anurag Dikshit, clicked by Sanjay Prasad. Picture: Anurag Dikshit
Us 'Famous' Five took an overnight bus to Goa and trekked the beaches. And lo and behold were we in for a surprise, when we saw Dr. Oberai sitting at a beach, fully dressed in his trousers and shirt, the spectacles and pipe in place, indulgently looking at his son who was frolicking in the sea. He looked at us, with possibly a bit of pride, and smiled.
My last encounter with Dr. Oberai was on the phone, a few years later, when he called me. These were the times of the old landlines, and I was surprised that he had traced me to my telephone extension at a corporate office in Connaught Place. First, he reconfirmed that I had shifted to Finance – gave me some words of wisdom about not using the knowledge gained at IIT. And then he asked for some inconsequential help, almost apologetically.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
A man who carried his position lightly, talked to you as a friend, and genuinely cared about his students' welfare. I have been privileged to have known him, albeit briefly, and admired him for his demeanor and his zest for life.
Anurag Dikshit (BT, ME, 1978-83)

The Rura Trot, August 15, 1982
IITK students, faculty and staff jogged 55 km from RPS Inter College, Rura to IITK, leaving Rura in the early hours of the morning and arriving at IITK twelve hours later. The team took breaks for drinks and lunch and stopped at the Hanuman Mandir at Panki before arriving on the campus in the evening.
The images show the team entering the IITK campus, and Prof MM Oberai greeting the runners with some well-deserved refreshments on the athletic field.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
Back at IITK, on the Main Drive, shortly after the gate
At the Athletics ground with some well-deserved snacks. Visible (facing the camera, L to R) are: Prof. MM Oberai, Bipin Bora (in the back), Ghosh Dada (Hockey Coach), Vinay Panchanadikar and Prof. Dhande (later Director Dhande). Pictures: Vinay Panchanadikar (1978-83)
849
Remembering Dad
Amit Oberai
At IITK, c. 1972
My father Dr. M. M. Oberai was a professor in Mechanical Engineering at IITK from the late 1960s through the early 90s, and I believe his greatest passion was for the students and community at IITK. I remember my dad from my childhood as a strong and loving man with a joke always at the tip of his tongue. Throughout my childhood our home was full of students visiting and the front door - and fridge door - were always open to them.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
He loved teaching and was a truly gifted teacher. In the 1960s he left his professorship at UCLA and returned to India to teach at IITK until his retirement. In 1969-70 he went on a one-year sabbatical to ETH Zurich in Switzerland. I was born in Kanpur upon my parents' return to IITK on September 10 that year, sharing the same birthday as my father. Although trained as a mathematician, his heart was in poetry and literature, and he was deeply into Urdu poetry. Later he moved to Delhi to be with his brother and extended family, and finally moved to the hills to the town of Bhowali near Nainital.
One of my greatest wishes was to live near him as he aged but that could not happen as he stayed in India while I was in the US. I am grateful that I was able to visit him every year. He passed away from COVID-19 on February 23, 2022, at the age of 89.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
To me, he was the most loving and self-sacrificing father imaginable. When I was little, I believed he could make anything happen. I grew up hearing stories of how he went door to door to all Punjabi family homes collecting what he jokingly called "gurdwara tax" to help build the gurdwara on campus, and how during strikes he would visit the Type 1 and Type 2 homes to check on families and make sure they were doing well. He always wanted to do everything he could for his family and for those he loved.
My memories of growing up on campus are filled with the friends and the activities we did. Life on campus as a child was simple and idyllic – the IITK campus was a unique and very special environment to grow up in. As kids we would play cricket in the open field behind the State Bank, and go to the student Halls to play ping pong and eat at the canteens there, usually at Halls 1 and 2.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
850
The Oberai Family. Front lawn, house no. 613, IITK campus, c. 1975
When I was little, my father and I would go regularly to the swimming pool on our bicycles. When I was very young, he would take me on his bike all the way from our house to the pool. I learned to play tennis at IITK, playing often with Dr Sriram's sons Gautam and Anil who are close friends. At home I had a croquet set my mom had brought and we would set it up on the lawn. Friends would come over and we would play UNO, croquet or badminton and drink nimbu pani (lemonade). Some of my closest friends were Dr TM Srinivasan's son Arun and Dr JL Batra's son Pankaj who was also my neighbor.
My mom had a group of friends at IITK and was part of a close-knit community. They would spend time cooking, sharing meals, and going for long walks together. I would join her when she visited her friend's house and sometimes on the walks. She was a great cook and even wrote a cookbook then. One of her passions was gardening. I remember we had a beautiful garden and how she would graft roses and win prizes from the plant nursery. I enjoyed going with her to the nursery and walking around among the plants and flowers.
[Context: IIT KANPUR DORA - ALUMNI NEWSLETTERS, GRAPEVINE (GVP) AND THE SPARK ISSUES > spark issue 17]
About the author:
Amit Oberai grew up on the IITK campus before moving to the US in the final year of high school in 1987. In the US, he was with his mother, Rachel, in Boston, and attended Brown University where he studied Physics. Later, he moved to Los Angeles for his PhD at UCLA in Bioinformatics and Computational Biophysics. He has settled in Irvine, CA, as a scientist at a small biotech called Antigen Discovery, with his wife Lily and their son Jay Mohan.
Amit enjoys oil painting as a hobby, something he has enjoyed from his childhood days on campus. Several of his paintings, including those of his father, have appeared in the UCLA art magazine The Beat. Some of these are republished here.
